# Rounds from R809: against this repository's `slicer_cli`

From R809 the oracle is this repository's `slicer_cli` (Bambu Studio
02.09.01.70, `references/BambuStudio` at `56a57d0`), not the 02.06.00.51 engine
the earlier rounds were scored against. Scores are in-order matched lines from
`scripts/parity/seq_parity.py`, on the job configs in
`tests/rust-engine/configs/` (`benchy-016`, `benchy-016-arachne`, `nu3mf` for
Majora), with both programs built on the same machine against the same Eigen
(5.0.1) and Boost (1.90) headers.

## How the scores are taken

`slicer_cli` is not reproducible run to run (about 900 lines on Majora), so from
R810 each fixture's oracle G-code is sliced once and kept, and every round's Rust
G-code is scored against that same file. The earlier state of the Rust engine is
re-measured against it through the gates (`NAME=0`), which is where the "before"
numbers come from. From R810 the STL jobs' settings bundle also keeps
`printer_model`, so `slicer_cli` treats the printer as the Bambu Lab printer it
is (main.cpp 11010-11023); without it the oracle left out the Bambu-only lines.

## Baseline

The Rust engine as it came from R808, against the kept oracle files:

| Fixture | In order | Content |
|-|-|-|
| Benchy classic | 30,486 (15.72%) | 41,423 (21.36%) |
| Benchy arachne | 32,769 (13.68%) | 55,458 (23.15%) |
| Majora | 1,512,438 (55.88%) | 1,745,378 (64.49%) |
| Majora, prime tower | 83.5% | |

## Rounds

| Round | What | Gates | Benchy classic | Benchy arachne | Majora |
|-|-|-|-|-|-|
| R809 | STL placed on the plate as `slicer_cli` does (`desktop_place_on_bed`, cli_model_load.cpp 280-378): mesh stored minus its bounding-box centre in f32, volume offset re-centred twice in f64, instance at the bed centre with a truncating `new_scale` shift (160 mm scales to 15999999), sliced through the exact composed f32 transform | `STL_DESKTOP_PLACE` (on) | 30,486 → 50,316 (25.91%) | 32,769 → 67,675 (28.27%) | unchanged (3MF path) |
| R810 | (1) Perimeter simplification read `enable_arc_fitting` as always on; the 02.09.01.70 H2D process profiles turn it off, and walls and gap fills are then simplified at the full `resolution` (PerimeterGenerator.cpp 924, 1521). (2) The 02.09.01.70 machine G-code placeholders `initial_no_support_hotend`, `current_hotend`, `initial_nozzle_id`, `nozzle_diameter_at_nozzle_id`, `long_retraction_when_ec`, `retraction_distance_when_ec` (GCode.cpp 2918-2937). (3) `slicer_cli`'s alias of `initial_no_support_filament_id` (main.cpp 486-580). (4) What `_do_export` writes after the last layer: power-loss line, final retract with a spiral lift, fans off, `M981 S0`, the role tag, `filament_end_gcode` through `writeln` (GCode.cpp 3548-3627). (5) `is_BBL_Printer` by `slicer_cli`'s rule. (6) Probe `PP=1`: per-layer checksums at the step boundaries, with a C++ twin kept out of the repository | `PG_ARC_FITTING_CFG`, `START_GCODE_2091`, `GCODE_TOKEN_ALIAS`, `END_SEQUENCE_CPP`, `BBL_PRINTER_RULE` (all on) | 50,316 → 124,330 (66.46%) | 67,675 → 126,359 (53.35%) | 1,512,438 → 1,512,509 |
| R811 | (1) The seam placer's occlusion mesh is built from the volume's own f32 mesh, through the volume matrix before the decimation and `trafo_centered()` after it (SeamPlacer.cpp 577-604); rounding the plate-frame mesh lost the low bits and flipped the seam between symmetric candidates. (2) In a wipe the pending acceleration comes after the wipe's speed line (GCode.cpp 496-510) | `SEAM_OCCL_LOCAL`, `WIPE_ACCEL_AFTER_SPEED` (on) | 124,330 → 137,832 (73.77%) | 126,359 → 136,442 (57.48%) | 1,512,509 → 1,512,563 |
| R812 | Farthest-point timelapse, new in 02.09: `compute_farthest_point` (GCode.cpp 4582-4733), the inline photo block of `generate_timelapse_gcode` (4735-4840), `check_and_insert_timelapse` after each extruded segment (7946-7973), no timelapse at the layer change (5604). `process_text`: template processing that keeps the whitespace around `{if}` tags as the C++ parser does | `FARTHEST_TIMELAPSE` (on) | 137,832 → 140,456 (76.41%) | 136,442 → 139,009 (59.31%) | unchanged (the X1C settings do not turn it on) |

| R814 | Fills, Benchy first. (1) A fill's non-extruding connectors (`ExtrusionPath` with `no_extrusion`, the hops of a monotonic-line top surface) are E-less moves on the linear branch of `_extrude` too (GCode.cpp 7993-7996); they were extruded. (2) `_extrude` does not simplify: with arc fitting off the one simplification is `simplify_path`'s at the print resolution (LayerRegion.cpp 1053-1069), also for sparse infill. (3) Top-one-wall: `fill_clip` is offset on the scaled value through the `float` parameter, and `top_polygons = diff_ex(fill_clip, inner_polygons, ApplySafetyOffset::Yes)` runs on the ClipperLib grid with the safety offset (PerimeterGenerator.cpp 1152, 1176, 1187). (4) `discover_sub_top_surfaces` and `stSubTop` with `sub_top_surface_pattern` (PrintObject.cpp 5839-5920, Fill.cpp 273-274, 556-558, 600). (5) Bridges of one overlap group that run 10 degrees or more apart become separate surfaces and share out their anchors: `separate_bridge_directions`, `partition_bridge_anchors`, the new `merge_bridges` (LayerRegion.cpp 372-637). (6) `polygons_covered_by_width` takes its delta in `float` (ExtrusionEntity.cpp); the fill boundary runs `simplify_polygons` after the Douglas-Peucker pass (`ExPolygon::simplify_p`); `Point::new_scale` truncation at the seam (SeamPlacer.cpp); the `float` radii of `discover_vertical_shells`; `FillGrid` reverses its polylines on odd layers (FillRectilinear.cpp 3127-3129); `intersection_ex(…, ApplySafetyOffset::Yes)` for a fill's no-overlap area (Fill.cpp 837). (7) Four arms that were parked because they lowered the score against the 02.06 oracle now raise it and are on | new, on: `NO_EXTRUSION_LINEAR`, `EXPORT_NO_RESIMPLIFY`, `TOPFILL_CLIP_FAITHFUL`, `SUB_TOP_SURFACES`, `BRIDGE_DIR_SPLIT`, `COVERED_WIDTH_F32`, `TAIL_SIMPLIFY_POLYGONS`, `SEAM_NEW_SCALE_TRUNC`, `GRID_ODD_REVERSE`; turned on: `VSHELL_REG_QUANT`, `FILL_NOOVERLAP_SAFETY`, `ARACHNE_SIMPLIFY_FAITHFUL`, `WIPE_TOWER_FORCE_TAGS`, `LINEWIDTH_BEFORE_SPEED`, `NO_COLLECTION_PRESPEED` | 140,457 → 148,085 (79.65%) | 139,010 → 143,944 (60.82%) | 1,512,564 → 1,524,497 (56.18%) |
| R815 | Bridges, from the probes of `expand_bridges_detect_orientations` on Benchy layer 2 until every stage agrees. (1) The floating edges of a bridge: closed polylines, the anchors grown as raw polygons with the miter join, one open-path ClipperLib difference (LayerRegion.cpp 353; ClipperUtils.cpp 841-849, 911). (2) `Line::normal()` is `(dy, -dx)` (Line.hpp 180), and the direction candidates are walked in the order of the `std::unordered_map` C++ keeps them in (BridgeDetector.hpp 89-113): two edges with opposite normals cost the same, and the order picks the sign. (3) `compute_principal_components` with the fused multiply-adds clang emits for its scalar `float` expressions on arm64 (PrincipalComponents2D.cpp). (4) `RegionExpansionParameters::build` in its own `float` scaled values, handed to the ClipperLib calls as they are (RegionExpansion.cpp 32-79). (5) `polylines_merge` swaps instead of reversing both and keeps the merge point twice (Polyline.hpp 236-246). (6) `closing_ex(Polygons)` over the raw polygons (ClipperUtils.cpp 598-602). (7) `diff_ex`, `intersection_ex` and `offset_ex(ExPolygons)` rebuild their expolygons with one union into a PolyTree (ClipperUtils.cpp 552-557, 641-654); the port ran `union_ex`, two unions, behind every one of them. (8) The pattern of a solid surface in the order of Fill.cpp 268-281: a bridge is rectilinear unless the top pattern is monotonic; it took the bottom pattern | new, on: `BRIDGE_FLOATING_CLIB`, `BRIDGE_DIR_HASH_ORDER`, `PCA_FP_CONTRACT`, `REGION_EXPANSION_PARAMS_F32`, `POLYLINES_MERGE_FAITHFUL`, `BRIDGE_CLOSING_FAITHFUL`, `CLIB_POLYTREE_ONE_PASS`, `FILL_PATTERN_CHAIN`; turned on: `BRIDGE_NORMAL_CPP` | 148,085 → 148,122 (79.66%) | 143,944 → 143,955 (60.81%) | 1,524,497 → 1,525,490 (56.27%) |

| R819 | The order the islands of a layer are printed in. `process_layer` puts every perimeter and fill collection into `islands[i]`, `i` being the lslice its first point lies in, and prints that vector front to back (GCode.cpp 5437-5527, 5922). The port printed the islands in the order they first appeared among the perimeter entities, which is the order the perimeter generator visited its surfaces in | new, on: `ISLAND_LSLICE_ORDER` | 149,451 → 164,807 (88.62%) | 158,538 → 204,849 (86.58%) | 1,796,767 → 1,847,143 (68.13%) |
| R820 | The order of the fills inside an island. (1) `Region::append` takes the entities out of every sortable fill collection (GCode.cpp 9125-9133), so `extrude_infill` chains the single paths of a region, not its collections (6983-7009). The port had this parked as `ISLAND_FLATTEN` because it scored far below zero: a path taken out of its collection was printed without its feature tag, width and speed. (2) Each thin fill sits in `fills` in a collection of its own (end of `make_fills`), so gap fills are chained with the other fills instead of following them. (3) The paths of a grid may not be reversed by the chain (`params.can_reverse = false`, Fill.cpp 832-833; FillBase.cpp 172-175) | on: `ISLAND_FLATTEN` (was parked), `FILL_GRID_NO_REVERSE` (new) | 164,807 → 171,768 (92.33%) | 204,849 → 212,289 (89.65%) | 1,847,143 → 1,847,625 (68.24%) |

The port compensated each region's slices by themselves in `make_perimeters`, after the untyped slices were backed up | on: `L0_EFC_MULTI_REGION` (new) | unchanged (171,768) | unchanged (212,289) | 1,954,872 → 1,959,722 (72.07%) |

From the G-code: (1) `unscale<float>(v)` is `float(v) * float(SCALING_FACTOR)` (libslic3r.h 112), and the float nearest 1e-5 is 2.5e-8 below it, so the width of a variable-width path (VariableWidth.cpp 66, 136, 203; FillFloatingConcentric.cpp 72) is one float step lower than a division by 1e5 gives on about a third of the paths, which moves the volumetric speed cap by 0.001 mm/min. (2) The vertical-shell cache over all regions of a multi-material print (PrintObject.cpp 2351-2386) computes its offsets in `float` over the scaled integer widths, and the perimeter shadow is one `offset2` of the lslices. (3) `apply_mm_segmentation` intersects the region's slices (subject) with the segmentation (clip), PrintObjectSlice.cpp 1921; the other way round Clipper rounds the crossings differently | on: `UNSCALE_FLOAT`, `VSHELL_ALL_REGIONS_F32`, `MMSEG_STOLEN_SUBJECT` (new) | 171,768 → 171,788 (92.34%) | 212,289 → 213,070 (89.98%) | 1,959,722 → 1,994,413 (73.35%) |
| R825 | From the map: `gcode/generator.rs` (the G-code text, its header and config block) moves into `g_code.rs`; output unchanged. (2) The end of `append_tcr` (GCode.cpp 1355-1373): the position after a tower visit is the tower's end position, the wipe path is the tower's own, and the retract there wipes along it and lifts at once at `prime_tower_lift_speed`; the port kept the last object position and wiped toward the object. (3) `extrude_loop` handed the seam placer the position times 1e6 instead of scaled | on: `SEAM_NEAREST_LAST_POS`, `TOWER_LEAVE_CPP`, `SEAM_LAST_POS_SCALED` (new) | unchanged (171,788) | unchanged (213,070) | 1,994,413 → 2,066,495 (75.89%) |
| R826 | The floating vertical shell. `slicer_cli` compiles the override layer's `Fill/FillFloatingConcentric.cpp`, not the engine's file. The override keeps the points of the self-intersection grid alive, so `detect_floating_line` runs; the port forced the detection off (`FVS_FORCE_NO_DETECT`, R801) to mirror the engine's file reading freed memory. With the override followed: `rebase_at` writes the closure flag at index n-1, and a finished path is flagged cfFloatingVerticalShell by the run it closes. In the exporter a flagged path takes the bridge speed and is bracketed by `; Slow Down Start` / `; Slow Down End` (`extrude_path` and `_extrude`, GCode.cpp 6930-6931, 7738-7741, 7874-7875, 8083-8085). | R827 | The gyroid sparse infill. (1) `params.density = float(0.01 * surface_fill.params.density)` (Fill.cpp 944): the product is taken in double and rounded to float once; multiplied in float, 10 % gives 0.099999994 where C++ has 0.1f. (2) `FillGyroid::_fill_surface_single` (FillGyroid.cpp 169, 175-176): the grid module `Point(2*M_PI*distance, ..)` is rounded by `lrint` (Point.hpp 179), and `ceil(bb.size()(0) / distance) + 1.` divides two `coord_t`, so the `ceil` does nothing; the port truncated the module and divided in double, which made one wave more per axis on a grid shifted by a few units. From the map: `gcode/smooth_speed.rs` (the smooth-speed helpers of GCode.cpp) moves into `g_code.rs`, output unchanged | on: `FILL_DENSITY_DOUBLE`, `GYROID_BBOX_CPP` (new) | unchanged (174,014) | unchanged (218,088) | 2,251,712 → 2,308,349 (83.21%) |

Between `; Slow Down Start` and `; Slow Down End` a line is not adjustable (216, 298-301), so its marker stays, with the F word when the feedrate changes and alone when it does not. (2) The tool-change block: the placeholder parser removes an `{if}` tag and nothing else, so a line that held only a tag stays as an empty line; the result gets `check_add_eol` and the `; FilamentChangeGCodeStart` / `; FilamentChangeGCodeEnd` markers (GCode.cpp 1122-1128); the filament-end text keeps its trailing blank and its empty lines. New: `scripts/parity/exact_lines.py`, every body line as written | on: `Z_HEIGHT_G6`, `TOOLCHANGE_TEMPLATE_TEXT` (new) | unchanged (174,109) | unchanged (218,276) | 2,564,965 → 2,570,411 (91.39%) |
| R834 | (1) Retraction inside infill. `GCode::needs_retraction` (GCode.cpp 8465-8486) skips the retraction of a travel only when `travel_inside_internal_regions_no_wall_crossing` holds, and that function has a third condition (RetractWhenCrossingPerimeters.cpp 113-198): the travel must not run within `wall_proximity_distance` (the inner wall's line width, or the nozzle diameter) of a wall for `retraction_minimum_travel` or longer. The port had the first two conditions only, so travels along a wall went without retraction and wipe. From the map: `travel_near_perimeters_too_long` was the one function missing in `gcode/retract_when_crossing_perimeters.rs`. With the three on, the replay's total is the oracle's (2851.220 s against 2851.21997), every per-line elapsed time is within 0.0006 s, and all blocks agree but 251 in the cruise feedrate and the last in its exit feedrate. | R854 | The time estimator, finished against the Benchy replay of R853, with the planner blocks now compared bit for bit. (1) The centripetal limit of `process_G1`, `float sin_theta_2 = sqrt((1.0f - cos(angle)) * 0.5f)` (GCodeProcessor.cpp:4339): `angle` is a float and libc++ declares `float cos(float)` in the global namespace, so the subtraction cancels in single precision; the port took the cosine in double. That was the 251 blocks. (2) The arc branch (`process_G2_G3`) never assigns `curr.axis_feedrate[E]`, it multiplies what the last move left (:5135), and never resets `curr.safe_feedrate`, it only lowers it (:5173-5179): a spiral lift after `G1 E-.02 F1800` inherits the retract's E feedrate and safe feedrate 2.5. That was the last block's exit. (3) Single against double: `AxisCoords` is `std::array<double, 4>` (GCodeProcessor.hpp:548) and both `delta_pos` and `State::axis_feedrate` are of it (:639-640), so `curr.feedrate * delta_pos[a] * inv_distance`, the feedrate factor `std::min<float>(f, max / abs)` and the axis acceleration limits (:4347-4376, :5131-5165) are evaluated in double and rounded once; `2 * PI * norm` of a `P1` arc (:4999), `sqrt(sqr(arc_length) + sqr(delta_pos[Z]))` (:5023) and `2 * M_PI + radian` (Circle.cpp:496-498) likewise. (4) clang on arm64 contracts `a * b + c` written in one expression: `speed_from_distance`, `max_allowable_speed`, `intersection_distance` (:126-143) and the dot and cross product of the centripetal angle (:4336-4337) are fused multiply-adds. The lines the replay cannot write are the first `M73 P0`, which is the G-code writer's placeholder, and the `M73 P100 R0` of the machine end G-code. Gates off the output is R853's byte for byte on all three; with the five on the bodies without `M73` lines are still those, and the in-order counts are 183,741 / 236,623 / 2,747,551, so they stay opt-in for the two arachne lines. ## Findings

- R1004. At the first tower-disabled tool change on the three-slot project,
  both writers had retracted 0 mm and had a 2 mm tool-change setting. Rust
  retracted 0.8 mm. With identical inputs, the revised writer reaches the
  native target on all 480 changes across four profiles covering 2 mm,
  0.25 mm, zero and disabled wiping. The 32 native writer cases also cover
  a prior retraction and absolute and relative E modes. The project gains
  422 in-order lines (9,819 to 10,241). The remaining template, temperature
  and conditional tool-selection steps are partial. The live profile loader
  also loses the absolute extrusion setting before the writer is initialized.
  `NO_TOWER_TOOLCHANGE_RETRACT=0` reproduces all six supplemental controls.
  All four standard fixtures match R1003 byte for byte with the gate on and
  off. H2D holds at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.

- R1003. The root CLI accepts `--temp` and `--bed-temp` on STL and Bambu
  3MF inputs (cli_command_line.cpp 562–563, main.cpp 7267–7327).
  All 144 typed native cases match, including empty, nullable and plain
  integer vectors. Five plate profiles and three projects produce the same
  motion and heating commands as their file-setting controls in each engine.
  The complete heating-command sequences agree across engines on seven jobs.
  With the tower disabled, the three-slot job emits 144 commands in Rust and
  624 in C++ with either source of settings. The 120 material changes account
  for the difference. Eighteen pairs of real jobs match in exit status and
  override or validation events. `CLI_TEMPERATURE_OPTIONS=0` restores the
  prior refusals. All four standard fixtures match R1002 byte for byte with
  the gate on and off. H2D holds at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.

- R1002. GCode.cpp 7106–7117 sends the prefix ending at the first NUL
  to both the file and the processor. R1001 wrote extra bytes in nine
  of 27 native stream cases. Every file, open/error state and processor
  move now matches, including a command written after each tested input.
  `GCODE_STREAM_C_STRING=0` reproduces all nine earlier outputs. An
  exclusive borrow ties the stream to its processor. The G-code class
  module now forbids unsafe code. On macOS arm64 the engine's unsafe-code
  diagnostics fell from 106 to 104, the target graph from 8,971 to 8,969,
  and the graph including host dependencies from 9,104 to 9,102. All four
  fixtures match R1001 byte for byte with the gate enabled and disabled.
  H2D holds at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.

- R1001. After identical XML callbacks, the line reader follows the pin's
  attribute order. A font-size change affects subsequent endpoints in the
  same element. The reader accepts the inherited font size, viewport and
  resolved transform and uses the existing cubic path builder. Across 32
  complete line documents, the open flag, every point and all bounds agree
  bit for bit with native on macOS arm64 and x86_64. The cases include
  coincident endpoints, omitted coordinates, repeated attributes, relative
  units and a transform. Full styled document import remains partial. All
  four standard jobs reproduce R1000 under both settings of the previous
  geometry gate. H2D holds at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863. The complete CLI also
  cross-linked for Linux musl on arm64 and x86_64 and for Windows GNU x86_64.
  Both Linux binaries sliced the geometry-option cube byte-identically to
  macOS arm64 (20,616 lines, including 10,853 motion commands). Windows
  execution remains open. The four target-specific unsafe-code counts are
  recorded in the engine README.

- R1000. NanoSVG's raw coordinate parser reads up to 63 number bytes and
  recognizes the unit at the end of the full token. The conversion uses
  the viewport axis, DPI or current font size according to the unit. The
  shared numeric reader also serves the existing polygon/polyline parser.
  All 319 native unit, raw-value and pixel-value cases agree on macOS arm64
  and x86_64. Six percentage conversions round differently between the two
  native builds. The safe crate and its four test targets also compile on
  Linux musl x86_64 and arm64, and Windows GNU x86_64. The converted values
  are available to shape readers. Full SVG import and full cross-platform
  CLI safety remain open. Four standard slicing jobs reproduce R999 with the
  previous geometry gate enabled and disabled. H2D holds at 4,614,342 and
  4,614,863.

- R999. The compiled compatibility cooling API collected mutable row pointers
  and dereferenced them to slow down a tier of extruders. It now collects
  exclusive references once and processes a sorted slice of them, preserving
  the caller's row order. The cooling module and its editor-based child now
  forbid unsafe code. The previous implementation fails that compiler check.
  Across all six permutations of three extruders and four disabled-slot
  configurations, the returned time and each row's elapsed time match native
  float bits before and after the change. The active G-code editor uses the
  child implementation. With no change to the slicing behavior, the four
  fixtures reproduce R998 with its geometry gate enabled and disabled. H2D
  holds at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863. Fresh macOS arm64 compiler audits count
  106 engine diagnostics (previously 107), 8,971 across the target graph
  (previously 8,972), and 9,104 including host builds (previously 9,105).

- R998. In two disjoint cubes, switching their three-wall and five-wall
  overrides swaps the native inner-wall move counts (10 and 20 at each of
  three heights). R997 produced 10 in both parts. A five-wall height band
  likewise produced 10 until its own region reached the live slicing path.
  The 3MF driver now uses the imported model's volume and height ranges
  to assign slices to the corresponding layer regions. The same pipeline
  handles hollow and 30% infill parts in either order, plus a hollow band.
  All 96 sampled feature/side/height counts across eight jobs match native,
  including the unchanged controls. All eight regional jobs reproduce R997
  byte for byte with `MODEL_GEOMETRY_REGIONS=0`. The four standard fixtures
  reproduce R997 both ways and the H2D scores hold at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.
  Painted projects and independently scheduled objects with different layer
  grids remain separate porting units.

- R997. The root driver now parses the three official height, wall and sparse
  density settings, validates the final typed values and applies the two
  legacy integer overrides last. In both engines the geometry flags produce
  103 layers on the test cube. With equivalent profiles, all 10,956 native
  and 10,853 Rust STL motions match each engine's flagged run. On the Bambu
  project the corresponding counts are 11,367 and 10,561. The legacy density
  option is published alongside the canonical density. Rejected legacy values
  retain the native event, key order and exit status. The 173 native parser
  vectors include still-refused nozzle and temperature controls. Forty
  supplemental calls cover scopes, precedence, diagnostics and gate-off
  behavior. All eight standard outputs reproduce R996. The H2D scores
  remain 4,614,342 and 4,614,863. Multi-region geometry remains partial.

- R996. On the calibrated Bambu cube, native model resolution and the Rust
  importer both returned five walls for object and part overrides of five.
  The live Rust region received the global count instead. The 3MF entry now
  passes the resolved wall count to the region when every part agrees.
  All 12 combinations in the native Print::apply vectors reproduce the
  complete motions of their effective-count control. The exported config
  retains the requested count. Changing the global count from three to five
  changes the plate control in both engines. For the five-wall job the native
  emits 10,738 motion lines and Rust emits 10,559. Different counts across
  parts, modifiers and height ranges still require per-layer geometry.
  Disabling `MODEL_REGION_WALL_LOOPS` reproduces all six R995 scope files.
  All four standard jobs match R995 byte for byte with the gate on and off.
  The two H2D scores remain 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.

- R995. All 120 SVG documents reproduce the native cubic points, bounds and
  closure on macOS arm64 and x86_64 under Rosetta. The first numeric
  difference occurs before geometry. NanoSVG saturates each integer and
  fractional digit sequence at the signed 64-bit limit and divides the
  fractional part by ten to the full number of digits. It consumes a long
  token even after its 63-byte buffer fills. A trailing separator after an
  unmatched X produces a final Y of zero. The parser takes the resolved
  element transform, which two documents check with an explicit matrix.
  Full style, viewport and document handling remain partial. No existing
  slicing mode calls this new function. The four standard jobs reproduce
  R994 with the previous STL gate enabled and disabled. H2D holds at
  4,614,342 and 4,614,863 against the two native runs.

- R994. All 344 native cubic-path vectors reproduce the point and bounds
  bits on macOS arm64 and baseline x86_64 under Rosetta. Clang contracts
  the first product in the arm64 transform. The native min/max helpers
  select their second operand on ties, including signed zero. The engine's
  path conversion reproduces 51 native lists of polygons and polylines
  from 21 SVG path strings. The safe NanoSVG crate and its tests also
  compile for Linux musl on x86_64 and arm64, and Windows GNU on x86_64.
  These are crate-level compile checks. Styled documents and the complete
  SVG import pipeline remain partial. This round adds a callable engine
  function. Standard slicing jobs do not read SVG paths. All four outputs
  reproduce R993 with the previous STL gate in both positions. Both H2D
  scores hold at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.

- R993. The native STL label stops at the first zero byte. With the label
  `A\0B`, Rust had written `B` at offset 2. This was the only differing
  byte in the 134-byte single-face file. The writer now uses the native
  label length and encodes each 32-bit value in little endian once.
  `BINARY_STL_LABEL=0` restores the previous label bytes. All 24 native
  files match, including counts 0, 1, 255 and 256, and labels that cross
  byte 80 inside a UTF-8 character. The TriangleMesh module now forbids
  unsafe code. On macOS arm64 the engine audit reports 107 unsafe-code
  diagnostics. The production target graph has 8,972 diagnostics, or
  9,105 including host dependencies. Both H2D scores hold at 4,614,342
  and 4,614,863.

- R992. The root Bambu timing command reads the embedded profile before
  motion and reuses the original file's published nozzle grouping. The
  793 native option checks cover cases where an earlier option consumes the
  timing token. The file-import generator records 28 states for seven inputs
  and four seed contexts, including an unknown producer, an empty diameter
  list and incomplete profiles. The two full Benchys report 2851.219970703125
  and 2873.909912109375 seconds. H2D reports 540256.4375 seconds after Majora
  and 499681.3125 seconds after itself, exactly as the native CLI does. All
  three pairs preserve their input hashes. The C++ viewer reads the back of
  an empty diameter vector for geometry in that edge case. Rust uses its
  existing default diameter there and retains the native zero filament count
  and timing. Dynamic viewer-only metadata and thermal post-processing remain
  partial. Both H2D scores hold at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863. The prior R991
  fixture driver toggled the older CLI normalization gate. A repeat with
  `EXPORT_PRINTER_KIND=0` reproduced R990 on all four files.

- R991, printer vendor. With identical moves and 12.5 seconds of configured
  compensation, a generic printer adds the compensation on a plain G29.
  A Bambu printer adds it within the M622 block. The processor reproduces
  the native times and substituted header lines across eight programs and
  keeps independently configured processors separate. On real STL and 3MF
  jobs the generic printer now gets the generic print-time header and omits
  Bambu's M981 command. The Bambu controls reproduce R990. The supplemental
  Cube still has a separate timing difference across engines. The two new
  dependency checks on macOS arm64 count 108 unsafe-code diagnostics in the
  engine, 8,973 across target code and 9,106 including host dependencies.
  Each export supplies its own vendor. Standalone Rust callers that update
  the public fallback `S_IS_BBL_PRINTER` now use `AtomicBool::store`.
  All four standard jobs reproduce R990 with the gate on and off. H2D
  matches 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 motion lines against the saved native runs.

- R990, command dispatch. The native table binds 47 spellings to 46 methods.
  G2 and G3 share a handler, T matches a prefix and virtual commands inside
  comments use the same trie. The engine now registers typed Rust methods
  and calls them directly. The public standalone trie still accepts caller
  IDs. Four new native programs exercise arcs, tool changes, temperature
  commands and virtual moves, including lower-case and unrecognized input.
  All earlier processor vectors, times and postprocessed output still agree.
  The clean macOS arm64 safety check reports 110 engine diagnostics, down
  from 111. The full host build has 9,108 across 85 packages, down from
  9,109. All eight fixture files reproduce R989 and H2D matches 4,614,342
  and 4,614,863 motion lines against the two native runs.

- R989, the production dependency ports. The engine and Voronoi builder now
  import the same Rust implementation of libc++ comparator sorting. Its native
  generator checks the exact order of equal keys in 36 lists, including the
  insertion-sort and pivot-selection boundaries. The Boost 1.90 oracle agrees
  on all 110,546 diagrams tested, including 106 Benchy slices. The existing
  oracle skips four slices with invalid geometry after coordinate rounding.
  Both crates forbid unsafe code. Two sort tests previously ran once in each
  Clipper instantiation and now run in the sort crate alongside the native
  ordering test. The four fixtures match R988 byte for byte in both runs.
  H2D matches 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 motion lines against the two native runs.
  One Clipper dependency remains open on macOS. On 250,004 angles, replacing
  Apple's paired sine/cosine call with Rust's `f64::sin_cos` reproduced every
  native pair at optimization level 3. At level 0 it changed 207 pairs.

- R988, legacy placeholders. The previous Rust CLI refused
  `--no-normalize-legacy-gcode` and silently rewrote templates by default.
  The checkout's C++ normalizer and Rust now agree on 27 settings snapshots
  and five exact event records. On a real STL the default reports three
  replacements across two keys. A 3MF reports two across the same keys.
  Preserving the spellings keeps both historical placeholder names usable
  by the Bambu exporter. The event follows early configuration checks and
  precedes print validation. All four fixtures reproduce R987 in both gate
  positions, including H2D at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.
- R987, the root command line. The native parser accepts `-- -calib.3mf`,
  one or two dashes before option names, and `--single-instance`. The Rust
  parser previously refused these calls. The new parser matches all 29
  vectors generated from the checkout's Bambu `parse_command_line`. A real
  dash-prefixed project produces identical G-code with the canonical and
  alternate spellings in each engine. The disabled gate reproduces the
  earlier help and refusals. All four official jobs reproduce R986 in both
  gate positions. H2D scores remain 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.
- R986, the first separate safe Rust dependency crates. The engine uses
  `eigen-transform` through its existing mesh API. Three native Eigen tests
  compare the output bits over 20,000 inputs each. The new `nanosvg` crate
  matches the pinned XML callbacks, including attribute truncation, NUL input
  and tag spelling. Four pinned SVG assets and 256 generated inputs produced
  418 identical native and Rust callbacks. SVG shape construction and OCCT
  meshing are still pending. Both crates forbid unsafe code. The four fixture
  files match R985 byte for byte, including H2D at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863.
- R985, the root Cargo package. A default Cargo build produces both previous
  binaries. The root CLI and parity tool preserve all 13 CLI tests and 43
  shell checks. All four standard fixture files match R984 byte for byte,
  including H2D at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in-order matches. A separate
  clean macOS arm64 build succeeded with both build-script C and C++
  compilers disabled. The root package forbids unsafe code. The production
  engine and its dependency ports are still being brought under that rule.
- R984, per-feature filament selection. All 768 input rows agreed on
  region slots, perimeters and fill roles before ordering. At layer 4 of
  the sparse cube both engines assigned sparse fill to slot 3. Only C++
  registered both tools. Rust now uses the parent fill collection to
  register the tool before flattening, then uses the same layer order
  for emission and the prime tower. The machine-start header and the
  writer also share the initial non-support filament. This removes a
  duplicate first-layer T command on four jobs. The 768 native layer
  orders reproduce the actual T commands on each layer of the six
  exports, including a uniform control. Counts of depositing moves agree
  for every outer wall, inner wall, sparse and solid fill, bottom, top
  and prime tower on each tool. With flushing into infill, 3,570 sparse
  moves use T1 and 22 use T2. In-order matches before and after:
  uniform 10,179 → 10,179, sparse 9,501 → 27,095, solid 9,065 → 14,640,
  three roles 8,765 → 29,498, tower disabled 8,542 → 9,817, and flushing
  into infill 9,788 → 13,924. With a tower the volume reported on the
  first active filament differs from native by roughly 3.25, also present
  on the uniform control. Without a tower the two volume entries are
  3,274.24 and 2,257.35 in Rust against 20,366.10 and 19,345.94 in C++.
  A marker in the filament-change script localized the missing purge on
  all 120 switches. Seven of the 23 earlier object and part jobs gain
  matches. The other 16 retain their bytes. Gate-off reproduces R983 on
  all 23, the six cubes and all four standard fixtures. Both gate positions
  reproduce all four standard fixture files. H2D remains at 4,614,342
  and 4,614,863. Mixed, support, object flushing, differing part scopes
  and per-layer assignments still require their respective registrations.
- R983, object and part filaments in a merged 3MF. The Rust Bambu 3MF model
  importer and region resolver reproduced all 15 native region
  assignments. The live geometry loader had discarded the scopes. The
  shared slicing region now takes the resolved slot when all printable
  parts and all three feature roles agree on one physical filament. The
  existing scarf check shares the model read. A 3MF slice test compares
  object, part, global and out-of-range settings with six native region
  configurations. Five live projects, including two parts with the
  same assignment, now match the native filament header, virtual tool and
  three AMS startup commands. Each gains five in-order lines (10,174 to
  10,179 on the cubes and 21,150 to 21,155 on the two-part job). The other
  18 controls and all 23 gate-off files match R982 byte for byte. Across
  both gate positions the four standard files reproduce R982, including
  H2D at 4,614,342 and 4,614,863. Distinct part and role filaments, layer
  overrides, painted regions and virtual-slot expansion still need their
  own region and tool ordering.
- R982, startup filament selection. The native layer tool ordering selected
  zero-based slot 1 for a cube assigned entirely to filament 2. Rust had the
  correct applied region but passed slot 0 to the startup templates. Both
  the provisional header used for statistics and the final header now read
  the ordered slots. The three physical-slot STL exports reproduce all nine
  native startup lines. A support-first ordering reproduces the three native
  lines with different slots for the machine and filament starts. Across 12
  supplemental 3MFs the gate restores the earlier file on every job. With
  it enabled, three exports select the correct filament and report 5,531.59
  mm³ where their volume lists were empty. Native reports 5,534.84 mm³.
  Unplanned multi-material jobs still need the per-feature ordering. In the
  wall-2, infill-1 cube its first layer is ordered 1, 0 by native and Rust
  still starts on 0. Both gate settings reproduce the four R981 files. H2D
  retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 matches in order.

- R981, global infill filament selection. On an unpainted 3MF with explicit
  wall, sparse and solid slots of 2, 3 and 2, Rust previously applied 2, 1
  and 1. Both typed profile slots and both effective slots now match native
  across 72 configurations, including inherited and out-of-range slots.
  Seven of 15 live 3MFs now match native on all three region assignments,
  up from five. The other eight require object or part settings. The four
  standard jobs and seven supplemental exports reproduce R980 with the
  gate on and off. H2D retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in order. On a job
  assigned entirely to filament 2, the exporter still starts on filament 1.

- R980, mixed-color preview. A two-layer program revisiting the same height
  had matching total and logical-layer times. The native result also had four
  durations for the preview slider, while Rust returned an empty list.
  Calling the existing per-height calculation at the end of finalization
  reproduces every duration. All 120 native snapshots agree across normal
  and stealth timing, both file-processing options, scarf and spiral modes,
  small Z changes, travel, pauses and repeated heights. On the same
  86,731-line native mixed-color export, all 381 durations per mode match
  bit for bit. The Rust slicer still omits the tool changes on that model.
  Both gate settings reproduce the four R979 files. H2D retains 4,614,342
  and 4,614,863 matches in order.

- R979, H2D temperature preview. The 1,400-line startup replay has 128
  preview moves. The first differing temperature was on line 838 at
  `T0 H-1`: native stored 220 °C and Rust stored 140 °C. Line 794 had
  requested 220 °C on physical extruder 1. Rust discarded the physical
  extruder map on config application. In a three-filament replay the
  previous command handler also missed the second filament on the same
  extruder and ignored the explicit I selector. Both input maps now
  survive profile application, including empty maps on reconfiguration.
  All 400 native cases agree immediately after the command and on three
  subsequent travel moves. Both gate settings reproduce all four R978 files.
  H2D retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 matches in order. On the same first
  15,000 H2D lines the enabled replay agrees on 11,001 moves for the
  compared IDs, roles, positions, temperatures, flow, fan and path fields.

- R978, scarf-seam config. Rust previously exported zero for a circle-enabled
  Benchy even though both engines read the circle settings as enabled. In
  six Bambu calibration projects the two importers agreed on all 68 scoped
  input rows, but the CLI discarded part, object and layer-range settings
  before deriving the flag. The CLI now uses the full importer when the
  profile or scoped metadata can enable scarf. An unmarked 3MF ignores
  embedded global settings and still honors the part override. A bare STL's
  unpainted triangles select filament 1. All 20 model decisions match the
  full and typed native configs, including a plate tool change, an unused
  filament, nonprintable objects and per-scope overrides. Across 14 STL and
  3MF jobs nine exported flags change to the native value. Every other byte
  reproduces R977, as does every gate-off export. H2D retains 4,614,342 and
  4,614,863 matches in order.

- R977, hardware-pause Benchy. The two processors already agreed on the
  normal and stealth totals and all three motion times of the 360-second
  example. When the native processor read `M400 U1`, it saved the current
  motion and layer. Rust first differed here. The finalized example now
  reports 33% complete, four minutes remaining, on layer 1. An identical-file
  replay of the 197,591-line Benchy also reproduces the native hardware pause
  on motion 151,378, its exact elapsed time in both modes and the public
  result of 99%, zero minutes remaining, on layer 300. The ordinary tagged
  stop list stays empty. H2D retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 matches in order.

- R976, color-change Benchy. A two-filament probe first differed at the
  active T0 tag. C++ recorded a color-change move and item with the supplied
  color, then assigned preview color 3 to inactive T1 before its next travel.
  Both moves now carry the native color. The generated programs cover the
  rotating default palette, malformed tool and color fields, consecutive
  delimiters, a 255-count wrap, time in both modes and filament volume.
  On the supplemental slice the native file is 4,639,237 bytes. The enabled
  gate reproduces every byte, and the disabled gate reproduces R975.
  H2D retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in-order matches.

- R975, pause-print Benchy. The same two-move probe first differed at the pause
  tag. Rust omitted the option move and stop time. With those restored, the
  first progress marker expands to the normal and stealth estimates and their
  stop countdowns (GCodeProcessor.cpp 675–703, 3597–3614). The file pass
  matches native text and move line numbers for 18 cases. Twenty observations
  before and after finalization cover the option move, item height and stop
  times through linear and arc extrusion (GCodeProcessor.hpp 1067–1100).
  On the full slice the native opening commands are `M73 P0 R49` and
  `M73 C49`. R974 emitted `M73 P0 R50` and omitted the stop. Enabling the
  gate reproduces all 4,639,219 native bytes. Disabling it reproduces R974.
  H2D retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in-order matches against the two oracles.

- R972, Majora layer 355. Both seam placers receive the same five-point loop,
  choose candidate 1434 in the same perimeter and compute the same concave
  bisector. A depth of 0.824215 mm and a half-angle cosine of 0.00000329415
  put the projected position at (−72581.578125, −161326.203125) mm.
  Both scaled coordinates exceed the range of the signed 32-bit coordinate
  used by the pin. At this point the live C++ writer obtained
  (1331776780, 1047248872). Standalone macOS arm64 and x86_64 builds of the
  same conversion returned the minimum signed 32-bit value on both axes.
  The native floating-point-to-integer conversion is undefined for this
  input. The Rust port keeps its wider coordinates and the resulting seam
  remains different on this fixture.

- R971, Majora. Both engines supplied the same 33 subject points and 41
  overhang samples at layer 173. The first difference came from the slab for
  color 8 at layer 172. The same 380 vertices formed four rings in C++ and
  three in Rust. Replaying the native lower outline in Rust reproduced the
  four signed distances bit for bit. The existing libc++ tie ordering
  reproduced the slab, the final painted region and all of layer 173 excluding
  M73. Across the four saved oracles it raised whole-layer consensus matches
  from 583 to 584 of 586. With that option enabled, the Majora in-order score
  fell from 2,864,040 to 2,863,853. H2D fell from 4,614,342 to 4,614,210
  against the first oracle and rose from 4,614,863 to 4,615,158 against the
  second. Both Benchys stayed byte-identical. The default keeps the R970
  scores. Across seven native runs the layer 435 outputs also vary.

- R970, Majora. At layer 24 the filter receives the same four paths in both
  engines. Rust had assigned degree 2 to each. Covering the full window
  yields native degree 3 and the wall runs at 30 mm/s. At layers 24 and 150
  the redundant F3000 and the F1800 word on the subsequent retraction both
  disappear. All four C++ runs reproduce every changed window. Excluding M73,
  layer 24 matches all four and layer 150 matches two. Full matches rise to
  583 of 586 consensus layers and 650 of 656 layers seen in any run. The
  whole-file unordered verbatim overlap falls by one to 3,022,796. H2D
  retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in-order matches.

- R969, Majora. At layer 91 the tool change requests 2.0 mm after the ordinary
  retraction has already taken 0.8 mm. Rust now emits the native
  `G1 E-1.2 F1800` and records the full 2.0 mm. Layers 91, 276, 590 and 594
  each gain that single extrusion command and match all four native runs in
  full when M73 is excluded. Agreement rises from 578 to 582 of the 586
  consensus layers. The estimated model time increases by one second and
  progress command placement changes. The whole-file verbatim overlap rises
  from 3,022,792 to 3,022,797. H2D retains 4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in-order
  matches against the two saved runs.

- R968, Majora. At layer 134, Rust had split the native collection of
  22.609158145468456 mm³ into two collections. Subtracting their volumes
  separately changed the residual purge volume by one float step. After the
  fix, all 26 changed five-line windows on layers 15, 134, 435 and 520 agree
  with all four native runs. Layers 15, 134 and 520 now match in full apart
  from M73, bringing agreement to 578 of the 586 consensus layers. The
  whole-file verbatim overlap rises by 26 to 3,022,792. H2D retains
  4,614,342 and 4,614,863 in-order matches against the two saved runs.

- R967, Majora. Excluding M73 progress, four native runs agree on 586 of 656 layers.
  Rust matches 575 of those layers, up from 517 at R966. Every changed site has one
  width tag in C++. The whole-file unordered verbatim count against the saved run goes
  from 3,022,768 to 3,022,766 of 3,024,752. Rust now emits 5,009 instances of width
  0.404381 and that C++ run emits 5,011. Removing 23 duplicates of this width accounts
  for the two fewer unordered matches. All 23 resulting sites agree with all four runs.

- R966, H2D heating and cooling. Replaying the single-worker C++ file through the Rust
  processor reproduces every temperature command and its placement in all 984 blocks
  (preamble plus 983 layers, with M73 and standalone M400 excluded when aligning commands).
  Replacing just layers 369–371 of the Rust file with those C++ layers reproduces the same
  result, including heating positions at layers 422 and 538 and the 114 °C command at
  layer 820. Three other C++ runs reproduce the Rust motion at layers 369–371. After
  excluding those postprocessor commands, all 983 Rust layers match at least one of seven
  native runs, including all 863 layers where the seven agree.

- R809: after placement, straight-wall coordinates agree to the last digit, so
  the frame is exact.
- R810, from the `PP` checksums on Benchy classic: the slices of both engines
  are identical on 299 of 300 layers (layer 76 differs; layer 0 differs in the
  region slices, the elephant-foot contour). After the fix the perimeters are
  identical on 298 of 300 layers and the gap fills on 248 of 254. The fill
  surfaces left by the perimeter step agree on 77 of 204 layers; where they
  differ it is by a few scaled units on single points. After `prepare_infill`
  almost no layer's internal or solid surfaces agree, and the fills agree on
  109 of 289 layers. That is where the next rounds go.
- R810: the new `stSubTop` surface type (the `sub_top_surface_pattern` setting)
  appears on three Benchy layers in the oracle and is not ported.
- R810, open after it (the seam and the wipe order were closed by R811): feed
  rates differ on walls whose geometry agrees. In the start G-code `{max(...)}` is not evaluated,
  `first_layer_print_min` is 144.9995 where the oracle prints 145, `;VT0` lacks
  ` H-1`, and the `M73` progress lines are fewer.
- R812: the 300 inline timelapse blocks are in the same place and have the same
  text as the oracle's. Six of them are followed or preceded by an extrusion
  whose E differs in the fifth decimal (`E.03564` against `E.03563`).
- R812, from `PP` checksums after each step of `prepare_infill` on Benchy
  classic: the typed region slices agree on 286 of 298 layers (internal) and
  168 of 169 (top). The fill surfaces already differ before `prepare_infill`,
  on 127 of 204 layers, by a few scaled units on a few points: on layer 3 four
  corner points are 10 units off (a safety offset applied on one side only, in
  the top-one-wall branch); on layer 29 three points are 1 to 4 units off.
  `discover_vertical_shells` then leaves 2 of 142 layers' internal surfaces
  equal. `combine_infill` and the new `discover_sub_top_surfaces` are not
  ported (the oracle has `stSubTop` surfaces on layers 38, 51 and 126).
- R813, looked at and left open:
  - Gap fill rings come out in the other direction and from another start point
    than the oracle's, so their lines carry other E values (Benchy layer 244).
  - `needs_retraction` did not change between 02.06 and 02.09; the oracle's
    extra wipes inside gap fill follow from the ring order above.
  - Majora, layer 300: the merged slice itself differs (1,386 points against
    1,367) and the region slices are 54 pieces against 47, so the wall
    differences there start before the wall generator. On layers whose slices
    agree, arachne junctions are 1 to 20 scaled units apart.
  - Benchy arachne, layer 100: the same paths, widths and flows as the oracle
    with coordinates 1 to 133 scaled units apart; the arachne input preparation
    is the first suspect.
- R814, what each part moved on Benchy classic / arachne, measured one at a
  time from the R813 state plus the gates of (6): E-less connectors +2,861 /
  +2,014; no second simplification +2,904 / +1,479; sub-top surfaces +76 /
  +256; the top-one-wall clip +303 / 0; the bridge split +20 / -21. The gates
  of (6) together +1,231 / +1,195, of which `FILL_NOOVERLAP_SAFETY` +825 / +786.
- R814, the parked gates, each alone against the new oracle (Benchy classic /
  arachne / Majora): `LINEWIDTH_BEFORE_SPEED` +215 / +14 / -91;
  `NO_COLLECTION_PRESPEED` +203 / +3 / 0; `WIPE_TOWER_FORCE_TAGS` 0 / 0 / +118;
  `ARACHNE_SIMPLIFY_FAITHFUL` 0 / 0 / +3,969. Left off: `GEO_SCALE_COORDT`
  (+335 / +876 / +1,713, but the 1e5 geo-clipper grid makes round offsets take
  minutes in the unit tests; the call sites have to move to the ClipperLib
  helpers instead), `TOWER_PRE_RETRACT` (Majora -14,271), `ISLAND_FLATTEN`
  (-25,803 / -4,104 / -84,842), `WRITER_POS_CLEAR_CPP` (Majora -1,311),
  `RASTER_QUANT` (-74 / +151 / +3), `MMS_PAINT_FMA` and `MMSEG_DIFF_CLIB` (no
  change).
- R814, from the `PP` and `FBIS` probes on Benchy classic after the round: the
  fill surfaces the perimeter step leaves agree on 197 of 204 layers (from 77),
  the top surfaces on 48 of 48 at every step of `prepare_infill`, the sub-top
  surfaces on 3 of 3. Still different: the internal bridges of
  `bridge_over_infill` (11 surfaces against 10, none equal), the bottom bridge
  of layer 2, and the fills on 155 of 289 layers.
- R814, looked at and left: `FillGrid::_layer_angle` is 0 in C++
  (FillRectilinear.hpp 75) and the port turns the grid by 90 degrees on odd
  layers; dropping the turn, or always turning, moves the scores by less than
  the noise between runs (-117 to +118 lines), because both sweeps of a grid
  are 90 degrees apart anyway. Sparse infill is 48% in order: on the layers
  looked at the polylines are the oracle's, started from the other end and
  taken in another order. With the same end point of the previous fill the
  oracle starts at the farther end of the first polyline, so the order comes
  from the chaining of the fill collections, not from the grid.
- R815: on Benchy layer 2 the wave seeds, the expansions, the bridge
  directions, the direction groups, every step of `partition_bridge_anchors`
  and the 104 bridge surfaces that come out are now the oracle's, and the
  bottom bridges agree on 4 of 4 layers after `process_external_surfaces`.
  Bridge lines in order: Benchy classic 110 → 383 of 1,587, arachne 206 → 500,
  Majora 2,351 → 3,574. The content match rises by 552 / 576 / 1,173 lines;
  the in-order score rises less because the fills printed after a bridge now
  start from another point (`FILL_PATTERN_CHAIN` alone: -56 / -77 in order,
  +388 / +414 content on the two Benchy jobs).
- R815: two results of the oracle depend on how it was built, and the port
  follows the build the scores are taken against (macOS, arm64, libc++). The
  sign of a bridge direction with tied costs follows the iteration order of
  libc++'s `std::unordered_map<double, …>`; libstdc++ orders it differently.
  The principal components of a fully anchored bridge are computed with fused
  multiply-adds; an x86-64 build without FMA rounds each product. Both are
  behind gates (`BRIDGE_DIR_HASH_ORDER`, `PCA_FP_CONTRACT`), so a Linux or
  x86-64 oracle can be matched by turning them off or by adding its arm.
- R815, open: `bridge_over_infill` (internal bridges on 11 layers against 10,
  none equal), the fill surfaces of layers 1, 71, 74, 99, 117, 125 (they follow
  the gap fills, which differ on layers 0, 1, 71, 72, 117, 196), and the fills,
  identical on 135 of 289 layers.
- R816, from the `BOI` probe on Benchy classic: the 20 bridge candidates, the
  anchoring lines of all 17 layers, and the internal bridges of all 11 layers
  are the oracle's. `prepare_infill` as a whole no longer adds a difference:
  what differs after it (internal surfaces on layers 4, 74, 93, solid ones on
  1, 4, 71, 93, 99, 117, 125) already differs before it, in the fill surfaces
  the perimeter step leaves and in `discover_vertical_shells`. The fills are
  identical on 173 of 289 layers (from 135). Bridge lines in order: 383 → 869
  of 1,604 on Benchy classic.
- R816: the grid layer angle that R814 left as noise was half of a real
  difference. With the angles also formed in `float` the anchoring lines agree
  to the unit; in double one point in twenty lands one unit off.
- R816, open: two layers' deep-infill areas (33, 46) are 70 to 90 units off on
  one vertex and one bridging angle differs in the tenth digit; sparse infill
  is still only 47% in order although its lines agree, because the fills
  printed before it end elsewhere.
- R817: of the 119 Benchy classic layers whose fills differed after R816, 107
  had identical grouped fill inputs. After the round the fills agree on 185 of
  289 layers. What is left on the layers looked at is the two fills that go
  through the arachne wall generator: the floating vertical shell
  (`FillFloatingConcentric`, 8 paths against 14 with other widths on layer 20)
  and the narrow internal solid fill (`FillConcentricInternal`, widths 0.66176
  against 0.66177, a ring started at the mirrored point on layer 22). They
  share the cause with the arachne walls of the other Benchy job.
- R818: with the same outline the port's `WallToolPaths` returns the oracle's
  toolpaths: of 2,680 calls with equal input, 2,694 prepared outlines agree to
  the point and 2,412 final toolpath sets agree to the unit. The other 275
  have the same lines and junction counts, with junction sums one or two
  units off (10 to 20 nm), which the G-code's three decimals do not show.
  The earlier finding that "junctions are 1 to 130 units apart" was the input,
  not the wall generator.
- R818: content match is now 88.5% on Benchy arachne and 77.1% on Majora, the
  in-order score 67.0% and 66.3%: a quarter of the matching wall lines come in
  another order. The order in which arachne walls are emitted is the next
  thing to look at.
- R819: with the islands in lslice order the in-order score is within two
  points of the content score on both Benchy jobs (88.6% of 90.7%, 86.6% of
  88.7%); walls are 97% in order on Benchy classic. What still comes in
  another order is the fills of one island: on Benchy classic layer 101 the
  oracle prints floating vertical shell, sparse infill, gap infill where the
  port prints sparse, gap, floating shell (`extrude_infill`,
  GCode.cpp 6983-7009).
- R819, Majora: content 77.1%, in order 68.1%. Floating vertical shell (25%
  in order), sparse infill (35%), top surface (50%) and bridges (42%) are the
  weak features; outer and inner walls are at 73% and 76%.
- R820, Benchy classic by feature, in order: walls 97%, sparse infill 91%
  (from 48%), solid infill 91%, bridges 92%, top surface 92%, gap infill 83%,
  floating vertical shell 75%. The floating shell is the one fill that still
  starts elsewhere: on layer 101 its first run begins at the mirrored point.
- R820: Majora gains 482 lines in order and loses 1,704 in content; its
  floating vertical shell (25% in order) and sparse infill (35%) did not move

- R842, corrections. R838's row said `35.0f / 1e-6f`; Bambu Studio's `SCALING_FACTOR` is 0.00001
  (libslic3r.h:58), so it is `35.0f / 1e-5f`, which a float rounds to exactly 3500000 where the double
  quotient truncates to 3499999 — the fix stands, the arithmetic in the row was wrong. R841's finding put the
  third-decimal differences down to a finer grid in C++; the grids are the same, so they are differences of
  arithmetic. R842's wipe is one; another candidate is clang contracting `a*b + c` into fused
  multiply-adds on arm64 in the arc fitter's circle formula (Circle.cpp:29-43), which Rust never does —
  to be checked with a twin probe.

  `GEOCALLS=1` first: every line it prints is a call site to port.

- R956, H2D layer 714 (zero-based, Z 143 mm). A single-threaded C++ seam probe and Rust agree on all recorded fields of 1,023 candidates and 27 perimeters after each of the five stages of `SeamPlacer::init`, from candidate collection through alignment. Both saved C++ G-code runs travel to X170.974 Y169.507 before the 0.378512 mm outer wall. Rust travels to X170.969 Y169.508. The next comparison is the input loop, the closest candidate in `place_seam`, and the first vertex after the split (SeamPlacer.cpp 1563–1627, GCode.cpp 6665–6682).

- R958, H2D layer 714. Two consecutive runs of the unmodified pinned C++ executable on the same 3MF produced both observed seam travels. One matched Rust for the entire layer (3,993 lines, 84,556 bytes, SHA-256 prefix `b51aa34d66b8c048`). The other matched the first saved C++ layer (84,557 bytes, `82cdc2264eeb3c97`). A placement probe found all 49 native seam and split-point pairs in the Rust trace. The saved runs remain the scoring references.

- R960, H2D. With the new gate, the matched motion-line counts rise from 4,613,532 to 4,614,341 against the first saved C++ run and from 4,614,404 to 4,614,862 against the second. In the complete layer comparison with four C++ runs, excluding M73 progress lines, 714 blocks (including the preamble) are identical across the four runs. Rust matches 709 of these after R960 and 700 before. The remaining five differ first at pre-heating or pre-cooling commands on layers 422, 538, 774 and 820, and at a floating vertical shell feedrate on layer 671.
- R961, H2D. The seven subject rings at layer 671 match C++ exactly. The five clip rings contain the same points, but R960 swapped the last two when rebuilding a polygon tree. Replaying the intersection output in native order changes the following difference from 515 vertices to the native 513. In-order matches increase by one against each saved run, to 4,614,342 and 4,614,863. Of the 714 blocks stable across four native runs with M73 excluded, 710 now match. Turning the gate off reproduces all four R960 fixture files byte for byte.
